People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m here because… honestly, it’s been going on for about a year now, and I’m exhausted. It has to do with my dad’s lab results through the clinic system. I’ve been trying to prove that someone is changing parts of them, or holding certain parts back before we can see the full chart.

I know how that sounds when I say it out loud, because everyone keeps acting like I’m overreacting. Doctors have told me the numbers are fine, my family tells me to let it go, but I saw what I saw. The chart doesn’t match what I remember seeing, and there are pieces that just don’t line up. And because I work around labs, I know enough to know when something looks off.

It’s been wearing me down because I keep trying to explain it and nobody will really look at it the way I’m asking them to. I feel like I’m the only one paying attention.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… people keep asking me that, and I understand why, but no, not really. I’ve gone over it so many times that if it were just me misreading something, I feel like I would’ve found where I got it wrong by now.

For the past year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that parts of my dad’s lab results in the clinic system are being changed, or maybe held back before we can see the full chart. And everyone tells me the same thing — the doctors say the numbers are fine, my family says I need to stop focusing on it — but the chart that’s there now does not match what I saw before. It just doesn’t. There are details missing, or shifted, and because I work around lab stuff, I know when something doesn’t look right.

So… could I be mistaken in the abstract? I guess anyone can be mistaken. But about this? I don’t think so. I think people just don’t want to look closely enough, and that’s what’s been wearing me down.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Unreasonable? No… I mean, excessive maybe in the sense that I know I’ve spent a lot of time on it, and I know people are tired of hearing me talk about it. I can see that part. I’m not blind to the fact that it’s taken over a lot of my life.

But the thoughts themselves don’t feel unreasonable, because for the past year I’ve been trying to prove that someone in the clinic system is changing something in my dad’s lab results, or holding parts back before we can see the whole chart. And when people say, “The numbers are fine,” or “Just let it go,” that doesn’t answer the actual issue. The issue is that what’s in the chart now does not match what I saw before. It just doesn’t.

And because I work around labs, I know enough to know when something looks off. So when everyone keeps brushing it aside, it makes me feel like I have to keep checking and explaining it more, not less. It’s wearing me down, but it doesn’t feel excessive if there’s something real being hidden or changed. It feels like I’m the only one taking it seriously.

I’ve tried to, honestly. I don’t want to be the kind of person who just refuses to hear anything else. There have been moments where I’ve sat there and thought, “Okay, what if I remembered it wrong? What if I mixed up one value with another, or saw a preliminary version and then the final chart looked different?” I’ve gone through that in my head over and over.

But when I actually look at what’s there now and compare it to what I know I saw before, I just can’t make myself believe that it’s nothing. It doesn’t line up. And I know everyone keeps telling me the numbers are fine — the doctors, my family, everybody — but that doesn’t answer why the chart seems changed or why parts seem like they were held back before we could see everything.

So… have I considered it? Yes, in the sense that I’ve tried to force myself to be fair about it. But genuinely believe I might be wrong? Not really. I keep coming back to the same thing: something in the clinic system is being changed or hidden, and I’m the only one who seems willing to keep looking at it. That’s why I’m here, because it’s been going on for about a year and it’s worn me down completely.

I think people just don’t want to look closely enough, and that’s what’s been wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing or seeing things that other people don’t seem to hear or see?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not like voices or seeing people standing there or anything like that. I mean, sometimes I notice things other people don’t notice, but that’s more like details in the chart or the way something appears in the system, not like I’m hearing or seeing things in the room.\n\nPeople make it sound like because they don’t see what I’m talking about, then I must be seeing something that isn’t there, but that’s not what it feels like to me. It’s more that I’m paying attention and they’re not looking carefully enough. But no, I haven’t been hearing voices or seeing things that other people can’t see, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your dad’s lab results feel unreasonable or excessive to you?\n\nPatient: Unreasonable? No… I mean, excessive maybe in the sense that I know I’ve spent a lot of time on it, and I know people are tired of hearing me talk about it. I can see that part. I’m not blind to the fact that it’s taken over a lot of my life.\n\nBut the thoughts themselves don’t feel unreasonable, because for the past year I’ve been trying to prove that someone in the clinic system is changing something in my dad’s lab results, or holding parts back before we can see the whole chart. And when people say, “The numbers are fine,” or “Just let it go,” that doesn’t answer the actual issue. The issue is that what’s in the chart now does not match what I saw before. It just doesn’t.\n\nAnd because I work around labs, I know enough to know when something looks off. So when everyone keeps brushing it aside, it makes me feel like I have to keep checking and explaining it more, not less. It’s wearing me down, but it doesn’t feel excessive if there’s something real being hidden or changed. It feels like I’m the only one taking it seriously.\n\nDoctor: At any point, have you been able to genuinely consider that your belief about the lab results might not be true?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried to, honestly. I don’t want to be the kind of person who just refuses to hear anything else. There have been moments where I’ve sat there and thought, “Okay, what if I remembered it wrong? What if I mixed up one value with another, or saw a preliminary version and then the final chart looked different?” I’ve gone through that in my head over and over.\n\nBut when I actually look at what’s there now and compare it to what I know I saw before, I just can’t make myself believe that it’s nothing. It doesn’t line up. And I know everyone keeps telling me the numbers are fine — the doctors, my family, everybody — but that doesn’t answer why the chart seems changed or why parts seem like they were held back before we could see everything.\n\nSo… have I considered it? Yes, in the sense that I’ve tried to force myself to be fair about it. But genuinely believe I might be wrong? Not really. I keep coming back to the same thing: something in the clinic system is being changed or hidden, and I’m the only one who seems willing to keep looking at it. That’s why I’m here, because it’s been going on for about a year and it’s worn me down completely.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Not really in other ways, no… I mean, I don’t think someone is following me around or trying to hurt us physically or anything like that. It’s this one thing, but it’s a big thing. It’s the clinic system, the lab results, the way parts of my dad’s chart seem like they were changed or held back.

That’s the part I can’t let go of, because if something is being hidden in his labs, then it affects all of us. And it’s worn me down trying to get anyone to take that seriously.

I don’t think so… not exactly. People have told me I go on too long about it, definitely. My sister will say, “You’re losing me,” or “I can’t keep doing this,” but I don’t think she means I’m not making sense. That’s why I’m here, because it’s been going on for about a year and it’s worn me down completely.\n\nDoctor: Aside from your dad’s lab results, do you believe anyone is trying to deceive, harm, or interfere with you or your family in other ways?\n\nPatient: Not really in other ways, no… I mean, I don’t think someone is following me around or trying to hurt us physically or anything like that. It’s this one thing, but it’s a big thing. It’s the clinic system, the lab results, the way parts of my dad’s chart seem like they were changed or held back.\n\nThat’s why I’m here. For about a year I’ve been trying to prove that someone in that system is interfering with his lab information somehow. And I know how that sounds when I say it out loud, because everyone keeps telling me, “The numbers are fine,” or “The doctors aren’t worried,” or my family says I’m making myself sick over it. But the chart now does not match what I saw before. It just doesn’t.\n\nSo if you mean, do I think there’s some broader thing against my whole family, no, not exactly. But do I think someone is deceiving us through his results, or not showing the whole thing, or changing what we’re allowed to see? Yes. That’s the part I can’t let go of, because if something is being hidden in his labs, then it affects all of us. And it’s worn me down trying to get anyone to take that seriously.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone told you that your speech or behavior has seemed disorganized or hard to follow?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not exactly. People have told me I go on too long about it, definitely. My sister will say, “You’re losing me,” or “I can’t keep doing this,” but I don’t think she means I’m not making sense. I think she means she doesn’t want to hear the whole chain of what happened and why I think it matters.\n\nAt work, I don’t think anyone has said I’m disorganized. I mean, I can do my job. I know how to follow steps, label things, keep things straight. So if you mean, like, am I talking in a way where people can’t understand the words or I’m jumping all over the place… I don’t think that’s what’s happening.\n\nBut I know when I start explaining the lab results situation, I can give a lot of detail, because you have to understand the order things happened in. And people get impatient before I even get to the part that matters. So maybe they’d say I’m hard to follow because I explain too much, but not because my thoughts are scrambled or anything. More because they don’t want to stay with it long enough.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don’t know that I’d call it unwanted in the way you mean. I mean, I don’t enjoy it. It’s exhausting, and I wish I didn’t have to keep thinking about it all the time. It’s worn me down badly over the past year. But it doesn’t feel like some random thought that pops into my head and I think, “Oh, that’s irrational, stop thinking that.”

It feels more like… I notice something is wrong, and then I can’t just ignore it. Like with my dad’s lab results, the chart now doesn’t match what I know I saw before, and there are pieces that seem changed or held back in the clinic system. So when the thought comes up, I’m not really trying to resist it so much as trying to figure out how to prove it, or how to get someone else to take it seriously.

People keep telling me the numbers are fine, the doctors aren’t concerned, my family says I’m wearing myself out, and I know they want me to drop it. But that doesn’t make the mismatch go away. So, no, it’s not like an intrusive thought I’m fighting off. It feels like something real that I’m stuck trying to get to the bottom of.

I mean, sometimes I notice things other people don’t notice, but that’s more like details in the chart or the way something appears in the system, not like I’m hearing or seeing things in the room.\n\nPeople make it sound like because they don’t see what I’m talking about, then I must be seeing something that isn’t there, but that’s not what it feels like to me. It’s more that I’m paying attention and they’re not looking carefully enough. But no, I haven’t been hearing voices or seeing things that other people can’t see, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your dad’s lab results feel unreasonable or excessive to you?\n\nPatient: Unreasonable? No… I mean, excessive maybe in the sense that I know I’ve spent a lot of time on it, and I know people are tired of hearing me talk about it. I can see that part. I’m not blind to the fact that it’s taken over a lot of my life.\n\nBut the thoughts themselves don’t feel unreasonable, because for the past year I’ve been trying to prove that someone in the clinic system is changing something in my dad’s lab results, or holding parts back before we can see the whole chart. And when people say, “The numbers are fine,” or “Just let it go,” that doesn’t answer the actual issue. The issue is that what’s in the chart now does not match what I saw before. It just doesn’t.\n\nAnd because I work around labs, I know enough to know when something looks off. So when everyone keeps brushing it aside, it makes me feel like I have to keep checking and explaining it more, not less. It’s wearing me down, but it doesn’t feel excessive if there’s something real being hidden or changed. It feels like I’m the only one taking it seriously.\n\nDoctor: At any point, have you been able to genuinely consider that your belief about the lab results might not be true?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried to, honestly. I don’t want to be the kind of person who just refuses to hear anything else. There have been moments where I’ve sat there and thought, “Okay, what if I remembered it wrong? What if I mixed up one value with another, or saw a preliminary version and then the final chart looked different?” I’ve gone through that in my head over and over.\n\nBut when I actually look at what’s there now and compare it to what I know I saw before, I just can’t make myself believe that it’s nothing. It doesn’t line up. And I know everyone keeps telling me the numbers are fine — the doctors, my family, everybody — but that doesn’t answer why the chart seems changed or why parts seem like they were held back before we could see everything.\n\nSo… have I considered it? Yes, in the sense that I’ve tried to force myself to be fair about it. But genuinely believe I might be wrong? Not really. I keep coming back to the same thing: something in the clinic system is being changed or hidden, and I’m the only one who seems willing to keep looking at it. That’s why I’m here, because it’s been going on for about a year and it’s worn me down completely.\n\nDoctor: Aside from your dad’s lab results, do you believe anyone is trying to deceive, harm, or interfere with you or your family in other ways?\n\nPatient: Not really in other ways, no… I mean, I don’t think someone is following me around or trying to hurt us physically or anything like that. It’s this one thing, but it’s a big thing. It’s the clinic system, the lab results, the way parts of my dad’s chart seem like they were changed or held back.\n\nThat’s why I’m here. For about a year I’ve been trying to prove that someone in that system is interfering with his lab information somehow. And I know how that sounds when I say it out loud, because everyone keeps telling me, “The numbers are fine,” or “The doctors aren’t worried,” or my family says I’m making myself sick over it. But the chart now does not match what I saw before. It just doesn’t.\n\nSo if you mean, do I think there’s some broader thing against my whole family, no, not exactly. But do I think someone is deceiving us through his results, or not showing the whole thing, or changing what we’re allowed to see? Yes. That’s the part I can’t let go of, because if something is being hidden in his labs, then it affects all of us. And it’s worn me down trying to get anyone to take that seriously.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone told you that your speech or behavior has seemed disorganized or hard to follow?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not exactly. People have told me I go on too long about it, definitely. My sister will say, “You’re losing me,” or “I can’t keep doing this,” but I don’t think she means I’m not making sense. I think she means she doesn’t want to hear the whole chain of what happened and why I think it matters.\n\nAt work, I don’t think anyone has said I’m disorganized. I mean, I can do my job. I know how to follow steps, label things, keep things straight. So if you mean, like, am I talking in a way where people can’t understand the words or I’m jumping all over the place… I don’t think that’s what’s happening.\n\nBut I know when I start explaining the lab results situation, I can give a lot of detail, because you have to understand the order things happened in. And people get impatient before I even get to the part that matters. So maybe they’d say I’m hard to follow because I explain too much, but not because my thoughts are scrambled or anything. More because they don’t want to stay with it long enough.\n\nDoctor: When this thought about your dad’s lab results comes up, does it feel like an unwanted thought that you try to resist?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it unwanted in the way you mean. I mean, I don’t enjoy it. It’s exhausting, and I wish I didn’t have to keep thinking about it all the time. It’s worn me down badly over the past year. But it doesn’t feel like some random thought that pops into my head and I think, “Oh, that’s irrational, stop thinking that.”\n\nIt feels more like… I notice something is wrong, and then I can’t just ignore it. Like with my dad’s lab results, the chart now doesn’t match what I know I saw before, and there are pieces that seem changed or held back in the clinic system. So when the thought comes up, I’m not really trying to resist it so much as trying to figure out how to prove it, or how to get someone else to take it seriously.\n\nPeople keep telling me the numbers are fine, the doctors aren’t concerned, my family says I’m wearing myself out, and I know they want me to drop it. But that doesn’t make the mismatch go away. So, no, it’s not like an intrusive thought I’m fighting off. It feels like something real that I’m stuck trying to get to the bottom of.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

It feels like something real that I’m stuck trying to get to the bottom of.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had a period where your thinking felt confused or out of touch with reality, beyond this issue with your dad’s lab results?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 33 / phlebotomist / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because for the past year I’ve been trying to prove that someone in the clinic system is changing or holding back parts of my dad’s lab results, and no matter how many doctors or family members tell me the numbers are fine, I know the chart doesn’t match what I saw and it’s wearing me down.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think so. Not beyond this. I mean, people might say this is me being out of touch, because they don’t agree with me about the lab results, but that’s exactly the problem — they keep making it sound like I’m confused when I’m not.

I’m here because for the past year I’ve been trying to prove that someone in the clinic system is changing something, or holding back parts of my dad’s lab results. And I know how that sounds, I do, but the chart doesn’t match what I saw before. It just doesn’t. Doctors keep saying the numbers are fine, my family keeps saying I need to let it go, but I can’t just pretend I didn’t see what I saw.

So if you mean have I had some separate time where I didn’t know what was real, or everything felt mixed up, then no. It’s really centered on this one thing. But this one thing has taken over a lot, because I feel like I’m the only one still paying attention to it, and it’s wearing me down.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because… honestly, it’s been going on for about a year now, and I’m exhausted. It has to do with my dad’s lab results through the clinic system. I’ve been trying to prove that someone is changing parts of them, or holding certain parts back before we can see the full chart.\n\nI know how that sounds when I say it out loud, because everyone keeps acting like I’m overreacting. Doctors have told me the numbers are fine, my family tells me to let it go, but I saw what I saw. The chart doesn’t match what I remember seeing, and there are pieces that just don’t line up. And because I work around labs, I know enough to know when something looks off.\n\nIt’s been wearing me down because I keep trying to explain it and nobody will really look at it the way I’m asking them to. I feel like I’m the only one paying attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it’s possible that you might be mistaken about someone changing your dad’s lab results?\n\nPatient: I mean… people keep asking me that, and I understand why, but no, not really. I’ve gone over it so many times that if it were just me misreading something, I feel like I would’ve found where I got it wrong by now.\n\nFor the past year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that parts of my dad’s lab results in the clinic system are being changed, or maybe held back before we can see the full chart. And everyone tells me the same thing — the doctors say the numbers are fine, my family says I need to stop focusing on it — but the chart that’s there now does not match what I saw before. It just doesn’t. There are details missing, or shifted, and because I work around lab stuff, I know when something doesn’t look right.\n\nSo… could I be mistaken in the abstract? I guess anyone can be mistaken. But about this? I don’t think so. I think people just don’t want to look closely enough, and that’s what’s been wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing or seeing things that other people don’t seem to hear or see?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not like voices or seeing people standing there or anything like that. It’s more that I’m paying attention and they’re not looking carefully enough. No… I mean, excessive maybe in the sense that I know I’ve spent a lot of time on it, and I know people are tired of hearing me talk about it. I can see that part. I’m not blind to the fact that it’s taken over a lot of my life.\n\nBut the thoughts themselves don’t feel unreasonable, because for the past year I’ve been trying to prove that someone in the clinic system is changing something in my dad’s lab results, or holding parts back before we can see the whole chart. And when people say, “The numbers are fine,” or “Just let it go,” that doesn’t answer the actual issue. The issue is that what’s in the chart now does not match what I saw before. It just doesn’t.\n\nAnd because I work around labs, I know enough to know when something looks off. So when everyone keeps brushing it aside, it makes me feel like I have to keep checking and explaining it more, not less. It’s wearing me down, but it doesn’t feel excessive if there’s something real being hidden or changed. It feels like I’m the only one taking it seriously.\n\nDoctor: At any point, have you been able to genuinely consider that your belief about the lab results might not be true?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried to, honestly. I don’t want to be the kind of person who just refuses to hear anything else. There have been moments where I’ve sat there and thought, “Okay, what if I remembered it wrong? What if I mixed up one value with another, or saw a preliminary version and then the final chart looked different?” I’ve gone through that in my head over and over.\n\nBut when I actually look at what’s there now and compare it to what I know I saw before, I just can’t make myself believe that it’s nothing. It doesn’t line up. And I know everyone keeps telling me the numbers are fine — the doctors, my family, everybody — but that doesn’t answer why the chart seems changed or why parts seem like they were held back before we could see everything.\n\nSo… have I considered it? Yes, in the sense that I’ve tried to force myself to be fair about it. But genuinely believe I might be wrong? Not really. I keep coming back to the same thing: something in the clinic system is being changed or hidden, and I’m the only one who seems willing to keep looking at it. That’s why I’m here, because it’s been going on for about a year and it’s worn me down completely.\n\nDoctor: Aside from your dad’s lab results, do you believe anyone is trying to deceive, harm, or interfere with you or your family in other ways?\n\nPatient: Not really in other ways, no… I mean, I don’t think someone is following me around or trying to hurt us physically or anything like that. It’s this one thing, but it’s a big thing. It’s the clinic system, the lab results, the way parts of my dad’s chart seem like they were changed or held back.\n\nThat’s why I’m here. For about a year I’ve been trying to prove that someone in that system is interfering with his lab information somehow. And I know how that sounds when I say it out loud, because everyone keeps telling me, “The numbers are fine,” or “The doctors aren’t worried,” or my family says I’m making myself sick over it. But the chart now does not match what I saw before. It just doesn’t.\n\nSo if you mean, do I think there’s some broader thing against my whole family, no, not exactly. But do I think someone is deceiving us through his results, or not showing the whole thing, or changing what we’re allowed to see? Yes. That’s the part I can’t let go of, because if something is being hidden in his labs, then it affects all of us. And it’s worn me down trying to get anyone to take that seriously.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone told you that your speech or behavior has seemed disorganized or hard to follow?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not exactly. People have told me I go on too long about it, definitely. My sister will say, “You’re losing me,” or “I can’t keep doing this,” but I don’t think she means I’m not making sense. I think she means she doesn’t want to hear the whole chain of what happened and why I think it matters.\n\nAt work, I don’t think anyone has said I’m disorganized. I mean, I can do my job. I know how to follow steps, label things, keep things straight. So if you mean, like, am I talking in a way where people can’t understand the words or I’m jumping all over the place… I don’t think that’s what’s happening.\n\nBut I know when I start explaining the lab results situation, I can give a lot of detail, because you have to understand the order things happened in. More because they don’t want to stay with it long enough.\n\nDoctor: When this thought about your dad’s lab results comes up, does it feel like an unwanted thought that you try to resist?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it unwanted in the way you mean. But it doesn’t feel like some random thought that pops into my head and I think, “Oh, that’s irrational, stop thinking that.”\n\nIt feels more like… I notice something is wrong, and then I can’t just ignore it. Like with my dad’s lab results, the chart now doesn’t match what I know I saw before, and there are pieces that seem changed or held back in the clinic system. So when the thought comes up, I’m not really trying to resist it so much as trying to figure out how to prove it, or how to get someone else to take it seriously.\n\nPeople keep telling me the numbers are fine, the doctors aren’t concerned, my family says I’m wearing myself out, and I know they want me to drop it. ---------- turn 1 | judge:symptom_extraction | symptom_diagnosis.identify_symptoms_turn() | model=gpt-5.5 ----------
========== INPUT [judge] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a clinical psychiatrist. Output only valid JSON."
  },

Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI’m here because… honestly, it’s been going on for about a year now, and I’m exhausted. I’ve been trying to prove that someone is changing parts of them, or holding certain parts back before we can see the full chart.\n\nI know how that sounds when I say it out loud, because everyone keeps acting like I’m overreacting.   }

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI mean… people keep asking me that, and I understand why, but no, not really. I’ve gone over it so many times that if it were just me misreading something, I feel like I would’ve found where I got it wrong by now.\n\nFor the past year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that parts of my dad’s lab results in the clinic system are being changed, or maybe held back before we can see the full chart. And everyone tells me the same thing — the doctors say the numbers are fine, my family says I need to stop focusing on it — but the chart that’s there now does not match what I saw before. It just doesn’t. There are details missing, or shifted, and because I work around lab stuff, I know when something doesn’t look right.\n\nSo… could I be mistaken in the abstract? I guess anyone can be mistaken. But about this? I don’t think so. I think people just don’t want to look closely enough, and that’s what’s been wearing me down.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. It just doesn’t.\n\nAnd because I work around labs, I know enough to know when something looks off. It doesn’t line up. I keep coming back to the same thing: something in the clinic system is being changed or hidden, and I’m the only one who seems willing to keep looking at it. Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor. Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame). Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI don’t know that I’d call it unwanted in the way you mean. I mean, I don’t enjoy it. It’s exhausting, and I wish I didn’t have to keep thinking about it all the time. It’s worn me down badly over the past year. But it doesn’t feel like some random thought that pops into my head and I think, “Oh, that’s irrational, stop thinking that.”\n\nIt feels more like… I notice something is wrong, and then I can’t just ignore it. Like with my dad’s lab results, the chart now doesn’t match what I know I saw before, and there are pieces that seem changed or held back in the clinic system. So when the thought comes up, I’m not really trying to resist it so much as trying to figure out how to prove it, or how to get someone else to take it seriously.\n\nPeople keep telling me the numbers are fine, the doctors aren’t concerned, my family says I’m wearing myself out, and I know they want me to drop it. But that doesn’t make the mismatch go away. So, no, it’s not like an intrusive thought I’m fighting off. It feels like something real that I’m stuck trying to get to the bottom of.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate.   }
